# Near-Optimal Quantum Algorithm and Complexity Analysis for Riccati Problems 

Jingyao Wang 1,8, Yanqiao Wang 2,3,4, Bowen Li 5, Jin-Peng Liu 2,6,7,†, Zhengfeng Ji 1,∗

> 1

Department of Computer Science and Technology, Tsinghua University  

> 2

Yau Mathematical Sciences Center, Tsinghua University  

> 3

Qiuzhen College, Tsinghua University  

> 4

Institute for AI Industry Research (AIR), Tsinghua University  

> 5

Academy of Mathematics and Systems Science, Chinese Academy of Sciences  

> 6

Institute for Applied Mathematics, Tsinghua University  

> 7

Beijing Institute of Mathematical Sciences and Applications  

> 8

TraverseQuantum Co., Ltd. 

Abstract 

We develop quantum algorithms that construct block-encodings of solution matrices for continuous- and discrete-time algebraic Riccati equations (CAREs and DAREs), differential Riccati equations (DREs), and finite Riccati recursions. The Quantum Weighted Riesz Method, unifying four kinds of Riccati problems, constructs projectors onto the solution graphs using weighted Riesz branching operators and recovers the solution matrices. Under the stated ac-cess and normalization assumptions, our algorithms for CAREs and DAREs have near-optimal query complexity Θ( R α) up to logarithmic factors, with the generalized singularity factor R

that reflects spectral separation, and the output normalization α. For DREs and finite-horizon problems, the query complexity has an additional initialization conditioning factor κinit . We establish product query lower bounds for specified input-oracle families, demonstrating neces-sary joint dependence on spectral separation and initialization or output scale. We also prove that selected-value promise problems are BQP-complete on explicit circuit-generated families of single-control discrete-time algebraic and zero-terminal differential Riccati equations. Appli-cations include classical feedback evaluation for linear–quadratic control, a query-complexity comparison for stable random-phase approximation Riccati equations in quantum chemistry, and heated boundary control with numerical validation.

Corresponding author: jizhengfeng@tsinghua.edu.cn

Corresponding author: liujinpeng@tsinghua.edu.cn

1.1 Background 

Riccati equations are an important class of nonlinear matrix problems arising in optimal control and quantum chemistry. In control, four formulations arise depending on the time horizon and whether time is continuous or discrete. The continuous- and discrete-time algebraic Riccati equations (CARE and DARE) determine stationary value functions and feedback laws in linear–quadratic regulation (LQR); differential Riccati equations (DRE) and finite Riccati recursions (RR) govern finite-time control with prescribed boundary data [1]. In quantum chemistry, Riccati equations also determine random-phase-approximation(RPA) amplitudes and have further applications [2]. A formulation underlies these nonlinear equations: the solution is closely connected to the solutions of a set of related linear matrix problems [3, 4]. The formulation admits a linear rep-resentation of the graph of its solution, the subspace of pairs consisting of a state and its image under the solution matrix. For a CARE, the stabilizing solution is represented by an invariant graph of a Hamiltonian matrix; for a DARE, it is represented by a deflating graph of a symplectic pencil [3, 4]. A DRE propagates the initial or terminal graph through a Hamiltonian flow, while a finite recursion propagates it through a linear-fractional lift [5, 6]. These representations connect algebraic solutions to selected spectral subspaces and finite-horizon solutions to the propagation of prescribed graph data. We study how block-encoding access to the input matrices can be used to construct a block-encoding of the solution. Here, a block-encoding is a unitary whose designated block approximates the solution matrix divided by a known normalization [7]. This operator output allows the solution to be used in subsequent quantum computation; a classical scalar feedback value is obtained by combining it with state preparation and matrix-element estimation. The algorithmic question is how spectral branch construction and graph recovery contribute to the cost of producing this output. We state the matrix access, spectral separation, invertibility, and normalization assumptions for each construction, and quantify nonnormal behavior through generalized singularity factors. 

1.2 From linear lifts to Riccati operators 

The Quantum Weighted Riesz Method has three stages, shown in Figure 1: Embed, QET, and Recover. Embed represents the Riccati problem by a linear matrix or a regular pencil and its graph data. The quantum eigenvalue transformation (QET) stage constructs the required weighted Riesz operators and uses them to form the graph projector. Recover acts on its block rows to return a block-encoding of the Riccati solution. 4Embed   

> Linear lift and problem data
> QET
> Weighted Riesz branch operators
> CARE / DARE
> Weighted Riesz as graph projector
> DRE / finite recursion
> Combine initialization to form decaying graph projector
> Recover
> Lower block row ×upper-block-row pseudoinverse

Figure 1: The Quantum Weighted Riesz Method. The QET stage constructs weighted Riesz branch operators. DREs and finite recursions combine these operators with initial data to form a decaying graph projector (DGP); CARE and DARE directly construct the limiting Riesz projector. Both routes use the same block-row recovery to return a block-encoding of the Riccati solution, with explicit output normalization and operator-norm error bounds under the stated assumptions. The graph of a Riccati solution matrix P is the n-dimensional subspace graph P = {[v; P v ] : 

v ∈ Cn}, which represents the action of P on all state vectors v. In the real scalar case, this graph is a line whose slope is the solution p. A projector Π onto the solution graph preserves this relation between the state and its image under P . With the coordinate embeddings E1 = [ I; 0] and

Figure 2: Spectral branches and weights in the QET stage. The top panels select the blue stable branches for CARE and DARE; gray points are unselected. The bottom panels include both blue and orange branches, with forward weights on the left or inside and inverse weights on the right or outside. Points are schematic spectral locations, and each circle marks |z| = 1 ; the DRE weights use ̂ HDRE = HDRE /α H .For a DRE or finite recursion, the initial graph generally has components in both spectral branches. Its forward evolution can grow on the right half-plane or outside the unit circle. Under the required branch-rank conditions, changing the graph coordinates lets us represent the same forward-evolved graph using inverse evolution on the growing branch and forward evolution on the other. In the DRE panel, this gives the weights eαH tz on the left and e−αH tz on the right, where z belongs to the spectrum of the normalized Hamiltonian ̂ HDRE = HDRE /α H . The recursion panel uses zk inside the unit circle and z−k outside. These scalar weights decay on their respective branches; the initial graph provides the relation between the two branches needed to recover the full evolving graph. For dynamic problems, the QET stage combines the weighted branch operators with the initial data to form a decaying graph projector onto the evolving graph. Its lower block row is the solution matrix times its upper block row, whose full row rank permits recovery by a Moore– Penrose pseudoinverse. The Recover stage therefore produces the matrix specified by (1.1) directly from its graph projector. The query analysis accounts separately for nonnormal amplification of the branch operators and for the conditioning and normalization of recovery. Contour–resolvent linear combinations of unitaries (LCU) implement the branch operators by approximating contour integrals with weighted sums of shifted inverses [10, 11]. The contour selects the spectral region, while the scalar weight specifies the action within that region. This construc-tion applies to ordinary matrices and regular pencils under their respective contour and inverse 6assumptions. For half-plane projectors of ordinary Hamiltonian matrices, we also develop polyno-mial selectors implemented through the Weyl linear-combination-of-Hermitian-matrices (LCHM) construction [12]. The weighted exponential and power operators are supplied by the contour con-struction. Section 2 gives the common operators and implementation assumptions used in the Riccati algorithms. 

1.3 Main results 

Quantum algorithms and feedback evaluation. We construct block-encodings of all four Riccati solution operators. Under the stated coherent access, spectral separation, and normalization assumptions, the matrix-oracle query bounds are 

QCARE = ˜O(RCARE αX ) ,QDARE = ˜O(RDARE αX ) ,QDRE = ˜O(RDRE κ(Π +R0) αP (t)

) ,QRR = ˜O(RRR κ(Π >R0) αPk ) .

(1.2) Each bound counts matrix queries for one call to a solution encoding with operator-norm error ε;

˜O hides logarithmic dependence on accuracy and the displayed parameters. These compact bounds assume bounded normalization-reduction overheads; the dynamic bounds also require calibrated initial-column scales. The actual output normalizations include the supplied solution-norm bounds and the scales of the constructed projectors. General bounds with all declared scales and re-encoding costs appear in the appendices. The generalized singularity factor R measures how close the shifted matrices along a separating contour are to singularity, relative to their declared scale. A large value means that some shifted matrix is nearly singular. This can reflect narrow spectral separation, which eigenvalue distances alone do not capture. For an ordinary Hamiltonian lift H,Proposition B.5 in Appendix B gives R = O(κ(H)) for the specified rectangles when the supplied normalization is comparable to ‖H‖ and the minimum singular value over imaginary-axis shifts is comparable to σmin (H). Here κ(H) = ‖H‖‖ H−1‖. Thus R extends the familiar conditioning of matrix inversion to the separation of spectral branches. The projector normalization records the geometry of the selected branch and the scale of its quantum construction. The projectors Π−, Π< select the stable CARE and finite unit-disk DARE subspaces; Π+, Π> select the right-half-plane and exterior-unit-disk branches used in the dynamic problems. For CARE and DARE, the complete upper block row has full row rank, and its pseu-doinverse is controlled by the solution scale. This dependence enters αX , together with the actual projector normalization. In particular, the complete DARE pencil projector includes the normal-ization of its right factor L. For DRE and recursion, R0 = [ I; P0] represents the initial graph, and 

κ(Π R0) measures how unevenly the projection represents its independent directions. When ΠR0

has full column rank, this relative measure is κ(Π R0) = ‖ΠR0‖/σ min (Π R0), while σmin (Π R0) is the absolute initialization nondegeneracy factor. The general implementation cost uses the actual en-coding normalization αΠR0 through the ratio αΠR0 /σ min (Π R0), which becomes a condition number under the stated calibration. Appendix B.1 develops these distinctions. We also evaluate classical LQR feedback from the Riccati solution. For scalar control, u(t) = 

−c∗P (t)x(t), where c = b/r for input column b and control weight r > 0. With vector-preparation 7costs accounted for separately, amplitude estimation gives the informal query bound [13]

One factor αP (t) enters the solution-circuit cost and the other enters classical scalar readout. We use one-dimensional control only to illustrate the procedure. Higher-dimensional controls follow the same construction and componentwise readout, with the total readout cost depending on the requested components. 

Query lower bounds and computational hardness. For each Riccati problem, we construct a two-input family with independent parameters 0 < μ 1, μ 2 ≤ 1/8 and generalized singularity factor 

Θ( μ−11 ). The algebraic families use a common actual output normalization αX = Θ( μ−12 ); the dynamic families have relative initialization condition number Θ( μ−12 ). A query-hybrid argument for explicitly specified smooth Julia input block-encodings gives 

qCARE = Ω( RCARE αX ) , qDARE = Ω( RDARE αX ) ,qDRE = Ω( RDRE κ(Π +R0)) , qRR = Ω( RRR κ(Π >R0)) . (1.4) Thus each family requires Ω(( μ1μ2)−1) queries for coherent solution block-encoding with its de-clared public normalization and common signal space. In the algebraic examples, ‖X‖ grows without bound as μ2 decreases, and the allowed error is at most a fixed fraction of the smaller solution norm. Our direct-projector algorithm attains an actual normalization of order ‖X‖ on these families: explicit small contours keep the raw projector normalization bounded, as proved in Proposition H.4. This construction uses no normalization reduction. The dynamic families have bounded solution norms, constant output normalization and absolute error, and evaluation time or step Θ( μ−11 log(1 /μ 2)) . For CARE and DARE, these lower bounds match (1.2) up to logarithmic factors, establishing near-optimal worst-case query complexity at the actual output normalizations achieved by our algorithms on these input-oracle families. For DRE and finite recursion, the lower bounds show that the generalized singularity factor and initialization condition number must enter multiplicatively in the worst case. We further prove that constructing DARE and DRE solution block-encodings with constant normalization and accuracy is BQP-hard, even with a single control input. The associated selected-value decision problems are BQP-complete on explicit circuit-generated families. We illustrate the benefits of our algorithms through two applications: electronic correlation energy estimation and optimal feedback control. 

Quantum chemistry. RPA correlation energies follow from a Riccati amplitude equation [2]. In our normalized family, u > 0 denotes the smallest eigenvalue of the electronic stability matrix. It tends to zero during molecular bond stretching [14], making the dependence on u−1 central to the cost of RPA calculations. Our method reduces the number of queries needed for energy readout on this family from ˜O(u−3/ε c) to ˜O(u−2/ε c). This improves the dependence on u−1 over the matched baseline in previous RPA work [2]. 8Heated boundary control (HBC). For a path of n thermal nodes with a heater at one endpoint, at fixed parameters and over a bounded time range, the spectral factor, initialization inverse, and output normalization remain O(1) in n, allowing the optimal feedback value u to be estimated to error εu and failure probability δ using O(ε−1 

> u

log(1 /δ ) log 3(e + 1 /ε u)) matrix and state-oracle calls, given coherent access to the current state with known bounded normalization. We also conduct numerical experiments to demonstrate the efficiency of our algorithm. 

1.4 Related work 

Classical CARE and DARE algorithms use invariant-subspace, generalized- eigenvalue, and matrix-sign methods, while Hamiltonian flows and linear-fractional lifts describe differential and recursive Riccati problems [3, 15, 4, 16, 17, 18, 19]. For state dimension n, dense Schur or QZ factorization costs O(n3), as does one dense matrix-sign iteration, and a K-step dense recursion costs roughly 

O(Kn 3). Large-scale and distributed-control methods instead exploit problem structure or finite-section modeling [20, 21, 22, 23]. The quantum part of the paper builds on block-encoding, quantum linear-system methods, QSP/QSVT, and qubitization [24, 25, 26, 7, 27, 28]. Related quantum-control and matrix-equation work uses linear ODEs, Riesz projectors, contour resolvents, and singular-value transformations [29, 2, 30]. We use these primitives to access the linear objects in the Riccati lifts. The analysis also draws on matrix-function perturbation theory, resolvent bounds for nonnormal matrices, contour quadrature, Faber approximation, Weyl LCHM, and generalized quantum signal processing [9, 31, 10, 32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 37, 12]. Related matrix-geometric-mean and oscillator results provide comparison points, but their lower bounds are not imported into the Riccati statements here [38, 39]. The present paper combines these tools with the four Riccati linear structures and graph recov-ery, and states the separation, resolvent, graph, and readout conditions needed for each construc-tion. Finite-section PDE examples and physical control readouts involve additional discretization, state preparation, trajectory generation, measurement, and classical reconstruction costs, which are treated separately. 

# 2 Preliminaries and quantum constructions 

We first specify the Riccati problems and the quantum output requested for each of them. We then define the weighted Riesz operators used to access their linear representations and give two constructions of these operators. This order separates the control problem, the operator to be computed, and the assumptions needed to implement it.

Definition 2.3 (Differential Riccati problem) . Given time-independent A, Hermitian G, Q, P 0, and a requested time t ∈ [0 , T ], find P (t) solving 

˙P (t) = −Q − A∗P (t) − P (t)A + P (t)GP (t), P (0) = P0. (2.7) The solution is promised to exist throughout [0 , T ].To express (2.6) in this convention, set s = T − t and P (s) = PLQR (T − s). The inputs to Definition 2.3 are then (−A, −G, −Q, P 0 = PT ), where A, G, Q on the right are the original control matrices. The physical control time t therefore corresponds to the algorithmic request s = T − t.This sign change is one reason for allowing general Hermitian DRE data. For the discrete finite-horizon problem, it is convenient to index the value matrix by the number of remaining steps: Vj (x) = x∗Pj x, with P0 = PT . One Bellman update adds a control step [1, Sec. 2.4]. 

Definition 2.4 (Finite Riccati recursion) . Given A, positive semidefinite G, Q, P 0, and an integer 

k ≥ 0, find Pk generated by 

Pj+1 = Q + A∗Pj (I + GP j )−1A, j = 0 , . . . , k − 1. (2.8) The positive semidefinite assumptions ensure that each inverse exists and that every iterate is positive semidefinite. Taking G = BR −1 

> c

B∗ recovers the k-step LQR value. For k ≥ 1, the first optimal control uses the next-stage value matrix: 

u∗ 

> 0

= −(Rc + B∗Pk−1B)−1B∗Pk−1A x 0. (2.9) Appendix A.1 verifies the finite-horizon identities, including the time reversal and positive semidef-initeness of the recursion. These definitions fix the desired solution, branch, and initial or terminal data. Spectral separation, graph invertibility, and matrix-access conditions are additional algorith-mic assumptions. In particular, invertibility of A is needed by the recursion lift used later, but is not part of the recursion problem itself. 

2.2 Inputs, outputs, and inverse primitives 

The preceding definitions specify matrices as mathematical outputs. Our quantum algorithms represent these matrices as blocks of unitary operators, with the normalization and approximation error stated explicitly. 11 Definition 2.5 (Block-encoding and requested output) . A unitary UT is an (αT , a T , ε T ) block-encoding of T if 

‖T − αT (〈0aT | ⊗ I)UT (|0aT 〉 ⊗ I)‖ ≤ εT . (2.10) Here αT > 0 is the normalization and aT is the number of ancilla qubits. The matrix obtained by multiplying the indicated block by αT is the decoded matrix ˜T . For any problem in Definitions 2.1– 2.4, the requested output is such a unitary for T = X, P (t), or Pk, with a declared αT and 

‖ ˜T − T ‖ ≤ ε. Rectangular matrices use separate input and output signal spaces, with dimension padding when needed. An exact input has εT = 0 ; an approximate input encodes a nearby matrix. Whenever an exact-input construction is applied to approximate data, we account for this perturbation in the final decoded error. One matrix-oracle query is one call to a supplied block-encoding or its adjoint. We count controlled calls at the same unit cost when controlled access is supplied. Access may be given to the original coefficient matrices or to an assembled lift; constructing the latter from the former has its own query cost. The common nonlinear operation on encoded matrices is inversion. We use the following inter-face to a QSVT inverse construction [7, 25]. 

Proposition 2.6 (Inverse and pseudoinverse primitive) . Let UT be an exact block-encoding of T

with normalization αT , and suppose the nonzero singular values of T lie in [γ, α T ], where 0 <γ ≤ αT is supplied to the inverse construction. Under the controlled-access and singular-value-transformation assumptions of that primitive, an encoding of the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse T +

can be constructed with normalization O(1 /γ ), decoded error εinv > 0 satisfying αT εinv ≤ 1, and query cost 

O

( αT

γ log 

(

e + 1

γε inv 

)) 

. (2.11) 

For square invertible T , the target is T −1. For a rectangular matrix, T + vanishes on the zero singular subspace. 

Appendix A.2 proves the scaling in this proposition. The singular-value threshold fixes both the inverse normalization and the cost of resolving small nonzero singular values. When γ esti-mates the smallest nonzero singular value within a constant factor, these bounds can be written in terms of ‖T +‖. For approximate inputs, Lemma A.2 in Appendix A.2 quantifies how small input errors preserve invertibility and affect the inverse. Pseudoinverse perturbations additionally require control of rank and the nonzero singular-value gap. The constructions below also use coherent preparation of LCU coefficients and coherent access to node-dependent inverse circuits or polynomial phases. Their query bounds assume these op-erations can be performed without measuring the node register. Coefficient computation, state preparation, arithmetic, and rotation synthesis are recorded separately from matrix-oracle queries. This convention makes the operator normalization and oracle cost comparable across the two con-structions. 12 2.3 Weighted Riesz operators for the four Riccati problems 

The common geometric object is the graph of the solution. For a matrix P ∈ Cn×n,

graph P = {[u; P u ] : u ∈ Cn}.

If the columns of [U ; V ], with U, V ∈ Cn×n, form a basis of this graph, then V = P U and U is invertible. Thus P = V U −1 is its matrix slope. In the scalar case, the graph is simply the line 

V = pU with slope p. A projector Π onto this graph sends each vector to the graph along ker Π :the projected component lies on the graph, and the removed component lies in the kernel. This direction need not be perpendicular to the graph. The complete projector encodes the same matrix slope through E∗ 

> 2

Π = P E ∗ 

> 1

Π. Its upper block row has full row rank, so the pseudoinverse recovery in (1.1) gives P .The block-encoding model supplies access to a linear matrix or pencil. For algebraic equations, a Riesz projector selects the target graph from the lift. Dynamic problems combine two spectral branches with the initial data to construct the evolving graph projector. Weighted Riesz operators select these branches and apply a scalar weight within each one. The weight is constant for algebraic problems and carries time or step dependence for dynamic problems. Fix a bounded spectral domain Dχ with positively oriented, piecewise smooth boundary Γχ.The contour lies in the resolvent set, winds once around the selected spectral component Kχ, and has winding number zero around the other components. Let g be holomorphic on a neighborhood of the closure of Dχ.

Definition 2.7 (Weighted Riesz operator) . For an ordinary matrix M , define [9, Chap. I, Sec. 5.6] 

Rχ[g; M, I] = 12πi

Rχ[g; M, L ] = 12πi

∮

> Γχ

g(z)( zL − M )−1L dz. (2.13) Regularity means that det( M − zL ) is not identically zero [4, Sec. 1]; the contour must satisfy 

det( zL − M ) 6 = 0 at every point. For g = 1 , these operators are the Riesz projector and the right deflating projector onto the selected branch, respectively. They need not be orthogonal. If g extends holomorphically to the full spectrum of an ordinary matrix, the weighted operator equals g(M )Π χ. The pencil integral selects finite modes; infinite modes do not contribute to this finite-domain integral. A DARE application must separately ensure that the selected finite deflating subspace represents its stabilizing graph. When L is invertible, 

Rχ[g; M, L ] = Rχ[g; L−1M, I], (2.14) whereas the pencil form remains meaningful for singular L. Proposition A.3 in Appendix A.3 proves these identities using the finite and infinite canonical blocks. 13 Multiplying by a right input R gives the common target operator 

Gχ = Rχ[g; M, L ]R. (2.15) Table 1 summarizes its use in the four problems. Algebraic equations use R = I to construct the complete projector. We use E1 = [ I; 0] and E2 = [0; I] as coordinate embeddings, and R0 = [ I; P0]

For CARE and DARE, stability selects a fixed target graph, and the unit-weight operator is the complete stable projector used in (1.1). Dynamic solutions also depend on the initial graph. Selecting one branch alone cannot preserve this dependence: whenever ΠR0 has full column rank, its columns span the same fixed subspace ran Π .The pseudoinverse preserves the connection to the initial data through the column coordinates. Here Π = Π + for DRE and Π = Π > for recursion. If ΠR0 has full column rank, then for any coefficient vector c, (Π R0)+(Π R0c) = c: it recovers the coefficients of the initial vector from its selected component. Multiplying by R0 recovers the full initial vector R0c, including its component in the other branch. The weighted operators then use this relation to assemble a projector onto the evolved graph. For DREs, forward weights act on the left-half-plane branch and inverse weights on the right; finite recursions use the analogous inside/outside branch construction. The next two subsections construct the required operators; the initial-data dependence and graph recovery are handled by the equation-specific analysis.

This completes the contour construction of the weighted Riesz block. Its error subsequently enters graph recovery, where the Riccati operator is obtained. For the unit-weight half-plane projector, the next subsection gives a second construction based on polynomial approximation. 

2.5 Weyl–LCHM construction of half-plane projectors 

We now construct the unit-weight Riesz operator for an ordinary matrix whose spectrum is sepa-rated by the imaginary axis. The target remains Gχ = Π χR, where Πχ = Rχ[1; ̂ H, I] selects the left or right half-plane, χ ∈ {− , +}. For CARE, R = I gives the complete stable projector Π−. A polynomial that approximates one on the selected branch and zero on the other approximates the Riesz projector when applied to the matrix. Weyl–LCHM provides a quantum implementation of this polynomial, giving another way to construct the same branch block. Let the normalized matrix satisfy 

‖̂H‖ ≤ 1, spec( ̂ H) ⊂ { z : Re z ≤ − ∆H } ∪ { z : Re z ≥ ∆H }, ∆H > 0. (2.26) All geometric parameters below refer to the normalized variable. If ̂ H = H/α H , the spectral separation and matrix-input error are divided by αH as well. The scalar identity underlying the polynomial is sign( z) = z(z2)−1/2, where the inverse square root uses its principal branch. The sign is 1 in the right half-plane and −1 in the left half-plane, so 

(1 ±sign( z)) /2 selects either branch [31, Chap. 5]. To approximate the inverse square root uniformly around the spectrum, choose 0 < r d < ∆H and rc > 1, and define 

E± = {z ∈ C : |z| ≤ rc, ± Re z ≥ rd}, Ωrd,r c = {z2 : z ∈ E+}. (2.27) 17 The contours Γ± = ∂E ± have positive orientation. Each spectral branch lies strictly inside its cap, so both contours lie in the resolvent set. Squaring maps either cap onto the same domain. The squared domain avoids (−∞ , 0] , so the same analytic branch of the inverse square root applies throughout it. Approximating this scalar function produces a polynomial selector. For a nonnormal matrix, however, scalar approximation error can be amplified when the polynomial is evaluated at the matrix. The next lemma quantifies that amplification through the Riesz integral and gives the error in the required right-multiplied block directly.

RDRE measures the relative proximity to singularity of the shifted matrices used to construct the weighted spectral branches. The full-rank condition means that projecting the initial graph onto the right-half-plane branch loses no direction: ran R0 ∩ ran Π − = {0}. Its smallest singular value is the initialization nondegeneracy factor, and κ(Π +R0) = ‖Π+R0‖/σ min (Π +R0) measures the relative imbalance among the projected directions. Rank loss makes these branch coordinates degenerate, even when the DRE solution exists; Example D.2 illustrates this distinction. The output normalization αP (t) records the cost of direct recovery from the upper projector block. It includes the supplied solution bound M , as specified in Appendix D.4. For the rectangular rule of Proposition D.4, with M, α R0 = O(1) and a calibrated initial singular-value estimate, Corollary D.7 gives

This is the Embed step for the DRE; Hamiltonian linearization also underlies earlier quantum approaches to finite-horizon Riccati problems [29]. It preserves the range of the Hamiltonian graph flow etHDRE R0, while expressing all time dependence through etHDRE Π− and e−tHDRE Π+. Their scalar weights decay on the selected half-planes, so the construction avoids directly encoding the exponentially amplified components of the forward flow. Consequently, E(t) represents the finite-time solution determined by P0, with the fixed kernel ran Π −.The projector property then connects the weighted Riesz blocks to the requested Riccati oper-ator. Since E(t) fixes every vector in the solution graph, its upper block row has full row rank, and its lower block row is P (t) times the upper one. These identities enable the direct recovery in the next subsection and control the recovery pseudoinverse through the solution scale. Appendix D.1 proves the theorem, and Appendix D.3 gives the finite-contour construction of all four weighted blocks.

The Hamiltonian lift represents the nonlinear evolution by the motion of a linear graph sub-space. The weighted Riesz blocks separate its two spectral branches and encode time dependence through decaying weights. The first pseudoinverse recovers the initial graph coordinates from their projection onto the right-half-plane branch. Combining the two branches therefore selects exactly the evolving graph determined by P0, with the left-half-plane subspace as its fixed complement. The second pseudoinverse chooses an input whose upper projector block equals a prescribed state x. Applying the lower block to that input then gives P (t)x, because every projected vector lies in the solution graph. Thus the recovery uses the defining relation between the two graph coordinates. The resulting output normalization enters both the construction bound and any subsequent feedback or measurement cost. The same argument applies to the discrete evolution considered next.

3.5 Algebraic Riccati equations 

The algebraic problems use the limiting graph in the continuous and discrete constructions above. Its projector is a unit-weight Riesz operator, so it can be constructed directly and passed to the same projector-block recovery. The resulting query bounds involve the generalized singularity factor and the actual output normalization. The limiting relations below specify the time direction and the connection between the discrete lift and the pencil.

Appendix F.3 proves the graph identity and construction bound. 27 4 Control outputs and Riccati applications 

The preceding sections construct Riccati operators. We first follow one control evaluation from the original coefficient encodings to a classical feedback value. A stable RPA family then quantifies how local inverse scales reduce query costs near a stability boundary. Finally, a thermal network with one control input and an arbitrary number of state variables makes explicit which physical and input-access conditions allow the gate complexity to grow only polylogarithmically with the state dimension.

The query bounds improve by factors u−1/2 for solution encoding and u−1 for energy readout. The source of this improvement is the difference between a peak inverse norm and its weighted 30 contribution along the contour: 

RCARE = sup 

> z∈Γ

(4.8) The largest inverse scale still determines the depth of the coherent inverse stage. Only a short part of the contour has this scale, so weighting each node by its own bound reduces the projector normalization, the cost of recovery, and the scale paid in readout. The upper-row pseudoinverse norm equals one for both implementations. For this nonnormal lift, the singular-value gap on the imaginary axis is u, whereas the smallest eigenvalue magnitude is √2u − u2; these gaps cannot be interchanged in the cost bound. Nodewise rebalancing for CARE is already present in [30, Definitions 5.8 and 9.10, Theorems 5.9 and 9.12]. The comparison quantifies local versus uniform normalization for the specified construc-tions. It does not establish superiority over that rebalanced algorithm or over optimized contour choices. The explicitly solvable two-mode family illustrates dependence on the stability parameter, rather than a quantum advantage for molecular instances. The thermal-network application that follows addresses a different regime: increasing the state dimension while keeping the physical scales bounded. 

4.3 Heated boundary control and finite-horizon feedback 

We consider heated boundary control (HBC), in which boundary heat input regulates the temper-ature distribution. Such problems have important engineering applications in thermal processing, including the control of cooling profiles in glass manufacturing [41]. The thermal network below provides a finite-dimensional example with a single heater at one endpoint. Consider a path of n ≥ 2 thermal nodes, with one local heater, nearest-neighbor conductance and uniform heat loss to the environment. The continuous model is 

˙x(t) = Acx(t) + bcu(t),Ac = −νI − κL, bc = e1,q > 0, r > 0, PT = 0 ,

The axes have different units, so neither a curve crossing nor a count ratio represents a runtime speedup. 34 5 Query lower bounds and BQP completeness 

We now establish two limitations on computing Riccati solution operators. For each of the four problems, one pair of oracle inputs gives a lower bound for the product of the generalized singularity factor and the prescribed algebraic output normalization or dynamic initialization condition number. We then embed quantum circuits into single-control DARE and DRE instances, proving hardness for solution encodings with constant normalization and accuracy. These two conclusions use different input families and access models. 

5.1 Product query lower bounds 

The lower-bound idea is to hide a visible change in the solution behind a very small change in the available input oracles. Fix public parameters 0 < μ 1, μ 2 ≤ 1/8 and a hidden bit c ∈ { 0, 1}.Each family below has R = Θ( μ−11 ). The algebraic families have solution norms and prescribed output normalizations of order μ−12 ; the dynamic families have initialization condition numbers of this order. These estimates hold for both inputs with uniform constants. Thus μ1 controls spectral separation, whereas μ2 controls the algebraic solution scale or a small initial component along the selected dynamic branch. Access is through the complete Julia input unitaries specified in Appendix H.2, including their adjoints and controlled versions. All other gates, numerical data, auxiliary registers, and signal spaces are common to the two inputs. In particular, the declared output normalization and the requested time or step are public and independent of c. We count the worst-case queries of one coherent circuit that constructs a correct solution encoding for either input. The input normalizations and contours are fixed as in Appendix H.2. For CARE and DARE, let 

Xc be the stabilizing solution and use the common actual output normalization αX = Θ( ‖Xc‖) = Θ(1 /μ 2) constructed in Proposition H.4. Require ε ≤ ‖ X1 − X0‖/8 in the accuracy range of the corresponding quantum problem. For these families, the solution difference equals the smaller solution norm. For DRE and RR, use αP (t) = αPk = 3 and ε ≤ 1/16 , respectively at the public time and step 

t = log(1 /μ 2)2μ1

, k =

⌈ log(1 /μ 2)2 log(1 + μ1)

⌉

.

These prescribed normalizations are part of the lower-bound tasks. After division by the common output normalization, the two solutions remain a constant distance apart. Solving the problem must therefore reveal which input was supplied, whereas each query changes the two computations by only O(μ1μ2). Accumulating a constant separation then requires Ω(( μ1μ2)−1) queries. 

Theorem 5.1 (DARE product query lower bound) . Under the above access and accuracy conven-tions, there exists a family of scalar instances of Problem 4 requiring 

qDARE = Ω( RDARE αX )

quantum queries in the worst case. The two factors can be made independently arbitrarily large. Informal proof. We construct two scalar DARE inputs whose finite pencil eigenvalues are r = 1 −μ1

35 and r−1. Their stable graph projectors and solutions are 

Π< = 11 + c + μ2

[

1 + c 1 + c

]

, Xc = 1 + cμ2

. (5.1) Appendix H.2 gives positive scalar coefficients realizing these data and proves RDARE = Θ( μ−11 ).The parameter μ1 fixes spectral separation, while μ2 fixes the solution and output scales. The two complete pencil input unitaries differ by only O(μ1μ2). Since ‖X1 − X0‖ = 1 /μ 2

and αX = Θ(1 /μ 2), the normalized solution blocks remain a constant distance apart under the stated error bound. Lemma H.2 therefore requires Ω(( μ1μ2)−1) queries to produce their constant separation, which is the claimed product. The complete coefficient, contour, and oracle calculations appear in Appendix H.2. 

Theorem 5.2 (CARE product query lower bound) . Under the above access and accuracy conven-tions, there exists a family of two-state instances of Problem 3 requiring 

qCARE = Ω( RCARE αX )

quantum queries in the worst case. The two factors can be made independently arbitrarily large. 

Theorem 5.3 (DRE product query lower bound) . Under the above access and accuracy conven-tions, there exists a family of two-state instances of Problem 1 requiring 

qDRE = Ω( RDRE κ(Π +R0)) 

quantum queries in the worst case. The two factors can be made independently arbitrarily large. 

Theorem 5.4 (RR product query lower bound) . Under the above access and accuracy conventions, there exists a family of two-state instances of Problem 2 requiring 

qRR = Ω( RRR κ(Π >R0)) 

quantum queries in the worst case. The two factors can be made independently arbitrarily large. 

The algebraic solution norms can grow arbitrarily large as μ2 → 0. For these same families, Proposition H.4 constructs the full projectors at constant raw normalization and applies projector-block recovery at an actual public output normalization αX = Θ( ‖Xc‖) = Θ(1 /μ 2), with no projector re-encoding cost. The lower bounds above use this actual normalization, so the product captures both spectral separation and the algebraic output scale, even for unbounded solutions. An arbitrary enlargement of the declared normalization would reduce the normalized output separation and is outside these fixed-scale lower-bound statements. The dynamic examples have bounded solutions and constant absolute error at the displayed time or step; their second factor instead measures the difficulty of initialization. Optimal dependence on additional normalization and re-encoding costs for general inputs remains open. 

Remark 5.5 (Scope of optimality) . The matching bounds establish worst-case optimal parameter dependence, up to logarithmic factors, for the specified oracle families and output conventions. They do not imply that our algorithms are efficient or optimal for every Riccati instance. For example, some problems with a poorly conditioned Hamiltonian lift H may still incur large query costs. These instances could effect the generalized singularity factor R and the stated initialization and normalization factors (see Proposition B.5). 36 5.2 BQP hardness of DARE and DRE solution encodings 

For the computational hardness results, the input is a finite uniform circuit description of a specified Riccati family, including efficient matrix-access circuits. BQP denotes bounded-error quantum polynomial time [42, 43]. We prove completeness for selected-value decision problems on the images of two explicit circuit reductions. A solution encoding with constant normalization and sufficiently small constant decoded error would decide the same promises by a constant number of matrix-element tests, as formalized in Lemma H.3. All solution encodings below include controlled access.

Theorem 5.7 (Single-control DRE hardness) . There exists a uniformly circuit-generated family of single-control, zero-terminal LQR instances with polynomial horizon T whose time-reversed DRE solution P (T ) in Problem 1 is BQP -hard to block-encode with αP (T ) = 4 and ε ≤ 1/100 . Both construction families satisfy the corresponding problem assumptions and admit the algo-rithms in Appendices D.4 and F.3, as verified in their proofs. 

Remark 5.8 (Computational difficulty of LQR values) . Theorems 5.6 and 5.7 show that LQR optimal values can encode general quantum computations even with one control input and positive semidefinite quadratic costs. A single known initial basis state carries the acceptance decision through its optimal cost, with a constant gap. This identifies an intrinsic computational difficulty in extracting value information from the dynamics. Here input size is the length of the finite circuit description, which can be much smaller than the state dimension. A randomized classical algorithm that estimates these optimal values to the stated constant accuracy in time polynomial in that description length would decide every problem in BQP with bounded error. 

# 6 Conclusion 

We develop a unified quantum approach to continuous- and discrete-time algebraic Riccati equa-tions, differential Riccati equations, and finite Riccati recursions. The Quantum Weighted Riesz Method combines spectral branch selection with the initial data to construct decaying graph pro-jectors and recovers algebraic solutions from their limiting projectors. Complete block-row recov-ery then produces the solution block-encoding, avoiding an additional square-block invertibility assumption and controlling the recovery pseudoinverse through the solution norm. This common construction preserves the initial information needed for dynamics and the stability selection needed for algebraic solutions. We establish matching upper and lower query bounds, up to logarithmic factors, on explicit algebraic input-oracle families at the actual normalizations achieved by our algorithm. The dy-namic lower bounds show that spectral separation and initialization costs must multiply in the worst case. Our single-control DARE and DRE reductions also establish BQP-hardness of constant-normalization, constant-accuracy solution encodings and BQP-completeness of selected-value promise problems on the circuit-generated families. Applications to RPA in quantum chemistry and heated boundary control demonstrate reduced query bounds from local inverse scales and efficient feedback evaluation in the specified thermal-network regime. Future work will focus on extending quantum matrix-equation methods beyond Riccati prob-lems, including coupled and higher-degree nonlinear equations. For matrix differential equations, an important direction is to combine suitable linear representations with quantum linear-system 38 algorithms (QLSAs), and to study linearization and time discretization when such representations are unavailable. The resulting methods should account for both approximation error and the cost of recovering the requested matrix solution or observable. Efficient input block-encoding preparation is another practical ingredient that merits further study.

In 2015 IEEE 56th Annual Symposium on Foundations of Computer Science (FOCS) , pages 792–809, 2015. 42 A Foundations and contour construction 

The constructions in Section 2 use a common sequence of operations: identify a weighted spectral branch, encode its shifted inverses, and combine them into a Riesz block. This appendix supplies the identities and error bounds behind that sequence. We first verify the LQR conventions and the inverse primitive, then establish the spectral meaning of the ordinary and pencil integrals. The contour proofs construct their finite sums; quadrature and input perturbations complete the approximation to the original Riesz operator. 

A.1 LQR identities and time conventions 

The finite-horizon formulations in Subsection 2.1 use two time conventions: physical time for con-tinuous control and the number of remaining steps for discrete control. The following identities verify the signs, matrix products, and feedback indices used there. They also explain why the positive semidefinite recursion remains defined when its iterates are singular.

A.2 Inverse primitives and input perturbations 

Proposition 2.6 applies the QSVT pseudoinverse construction to a matrix with arbitrary input normalization. The proof below makes that rescaling explicit, so that the same primitive can be used at each contour node.

The factorization T +∆ T = T (I+E) proves invertibility and the first bound. The resolvent identity 

(T + ∆ T )−1 − T −1 = −(T + ∆ T )−1∆T T −1

then yields the second bound by submultiplicativity of the norm. The smallness condition supplies both invertibility of the perturbed shift and a bound on its inverse norm. We will apply it node by node after establishing the exact contour construction. 

A.3 Spectral meaning of weighted Riesz operators 

Inversion provides access to resolvents, but the branch interpretation comes from their contour integral. The next proposition justifies the ordinary and pencil targets in Definition 2.7. It allows arbitrary Jordan structure and a singular pencil coefficient L.

The argument requires holomorphy only on the selected domain; it therefore also permits a bounded annular domain for an exterior branch weighted by z−k. Such a domain excludes zero and its inner boundary has clockwise orientation. The integral identifies the selected spectral subspace; its relation to a particular Riccati graph uses the assumptions of the corresponding equation-specific construction.

A.5 Quadrature error 

The implementation bounds above take the nodes and weights as supplied data. To connect them to the continuous Riesz target, one needs a quadrature error bound. Analytic parametrizations give a useful sufficient condition for logarithmic dependence of the node count on inverse precision, as in the classical trapezoidal-rule analysis [45].

The assumption concerns the full integrand: its analytic strip must avoid resolvent poles and singularities of the scalar weight. The quantities a and sup | Im t|≤ a ‖F (t)‖ can depend on spectral geometry, nonnormality, time, or the recursion index. Logarithmic dependence on inverse precision holds with those data fixed. For a general piecewise smooth contour, the implementation proposi-tions still apply to any supplied quadrature rule, but its error requires a separate bound; piecewise smoothness alone does not imply (A.9). 

A.6 Approximate inputs and total error

It does not require the perturbed matrices to define the same spectral branch on the entire contour.

Any subsequent graph recovery propagates this branch error through the equation-specific inverse or projector-block pseudoinverse construction. 51 B Complexity factors, spectral separation, and coefficient bounds 

The query bounds include the cost of constructing a graph projector, the normalization of the recovered Riccati matrix, and, for a dynamic problem, the conditioning of its initialization. We first explain these quantities, then relate spectral separation to contours and ordinary condition numbers. Coefficient bounds give sufficient conditions for estimating them before solving the Riccati equation. 

B.1 How the factors enter the query complexity 

In the contour construction, each node requires an inverse of a shifted matrix. The generalized singularity factor measures the largest inverse scale that these calls resolve. Both algebraic and dynamic recovery then multiply the lower block row of the complete graph projector by the pseu-doinverse of its upper block row. For a graph with slope X, Lemma D.3 gives 

X = ( E∗

˜O(rΠRαX ) , ˜O

rΠ defined in Appendix F.1. The compact main bound assumes rΠ = O(1) . The second describes the dynamic construction, with αP = αP (t) for DRE and αP = αPk for RR. It still depends on the stated initial-column calibration, normalization changes, inverse-error conditions, and supplied solution-norm bound. The expanded algebraic precision dependence appears in (F.9) and (F.24). An LCU normalization records the magnitudes of its summands before cancellation and can exceed the norm of their sum. For the specific positive-weight rectangle and unit-circle rules in Lemmas F.2 and F.6, the raw full-projector scales satisfy 

αraw Π− = αCARE = O(RCARE ), αraw Π< = αLαDARE = O(RDARE ),

uniformly in dimension and quadrature accuracy. The pencil scale includes the right factor L.These estimates concern the specified quadrature rules; the general query bounds include the actual supplied scale and the cost of any normalization reduction. For direct LCU recovery, a supplied bound ‖X‖ ≤ u gives the output scale αX = 8 αΠ

√1 + u2, as in (F.3). Conservatism in either supplied bound remains in the implementation cost. The corresponding full-projector errors and node counts are given in Propositions F.3 and F.7. We use the name generalized singularity factor both for an unnormalized inverse norm and for the quantity that includes its shifted-input scale. For an ordinary shift these are ‖(zI − M )−1‖ and 

(|z| + αM )‖(zI − M )−1‖, respectively; the corresponding pencil scale is |z|αL + αM . The formula specifies which quantity is meant. Their geometric meaning follows from the standard distance characterization of the smallest singular value [32]. 52 Lemma B.1 (Distance to rank deficiency) . For T ∈ Cm×n, m ≥ n,

σmin (T ) = inf

For a pencil, replace the normalized shift by (zL − M )/(|z|αL + αM ).Proof. A perturbation of norm less than σmin (T ) preserves full column rank by the singular-value perturbation inequality. For unit singular vectors with T v = σmin (T )u, the perturbation 

−σmin (T )uv ∗ attains rank deficiency. If T is already rank deficient, use the zero perturbation. Apply this identity to each normalized shift and use ‖T −1‖ = 1 /σ min (T ) for an invertible square matrix. Thus a large generalized singularity factor means that some shift is close to singularity at the stated scale. This is a statement about the shifted matrices; it is not a condition number for structured perturbations of the original Riccati data. That interpretation would require a specified perturbation model for the coefficients. The dynamic construction additionally depends on its initial branch column. Its absolute and relative nondegeneracy factors play different roles in the inverse implementation. 

Definition B.2 (Initialization nondegeneracy factors) . For a selected dynamic projector, the ab-solute initialization nondegeneracy factor is σmin (Π sel R0), where R0 = [ I; P0]. When this column has full rank, its relative initialization nondegeneracy factor is 

σmin (Π sel R0)

‖Πsel R0‖ = κ(Π sel R0)−1. (B.4) The selected projectors for DRE and RR are respectively Π+ and Π>, with the branch conventions in Table 1. An absolute lower bound on σmin (Π sel R0) does not remove the column norm from its condition number. The initialization pseudoinverse depends on its actual encoding normalization divided by 

σmin (Π sel R0); this ratio equals the condition number only under the corresponding norm calibration. At rank deficiency the Moore–Penrose inverse can still have finite norm; these reciprocal identities require full column rank. The full-projector recovery bound above depends on the graph slope, while the actual projector normalization can also reflect the angle between its range and kernel. For a nontrivial projector, 

‖Π‖ = 1 / sin θmin (ran Π , ker Π) [8]. This exact norm is a lower bound on an exact encoding scale; it does not give a free normalization reduction. The following geometry translates coefficient bounds on the discarded graph into initialization bounds.

Orthonormal frames for the two graph complements are [−X; I]( I + X2)−1/2 and [I; Y ]( I + Y 2)−1/2. Multiplication by their adjoints proves the distance identities. κ(Π sel R0) ≤ ‖ Πsel ‖√1 + p2/σ min (Π sel R0). Using (B.7) in this estimate can introduce another generalized singularity factor; it does not justify discarding the column norm from (B.1). A supplied positive lower bound on an initialization singular value can be used conservatively in its inverse threshold and error budget. Its reciprocal remains in the cost unless it approximates the actual value within a constant factor. The threshold also divides the supplied bound by the encoding normalization, as in Proposition 2.6. For algebraic recovery, conservatism in a supplied solution-norm bound instead enters (F.3) and its precision budget. With uniform bounds on the spectral factors, actual normalizations, supplied solution norms, and re-encoding overheads, the two algebraic theorems give O(log 2(e+1 /ε )) matrix queries. Dimension can enter these parameters and each oracle’s implementation cost; it is not an additional multiplier in the count of supplied-oracle calls. 

B.2 Spectral separation, contours, and condition numbers 

The usual condition number of an invertible H examines its singular values at the zero shift. Selecting a half-plane branch instead requires separation along the imaginary axis. The following bounds turn that separation into finite contours and keep the supplied encoding scale explicit. 

Theorem B.4 (Half-plane factors on finite rectangles) . Let H have no imaginary-axis spectrum and let αH ≥ ‖ H‖. Set 

∆ax = inf  

> ω∈R

σmin (i ωI − H) > 0. (B.8) 

For 0 < τ < 1, the positively oriented boundaries of 

{x + i y : τ ∆ax ≤ x ≤ 2αH , |y| ≤ 2αH }, {x + i y : −2αH ≤ x ≤ − τ ∆ax , |y| ≤ 2αH } (B.9) 54 enclose exactly the corresponding half-plane branches. On both contours the inverse norm is at most 

1/((1 − τ )∆ ax ), and their maximum generalized singularity factor satisfies 

αH

(1 + τ )∆ ax 

≤ R H,τ ≤ 1 + 2 √21 − ταH

∆ax 

. (B.10) 

Here RH,τ is the supremum of (|z| + αH )‖(zI − H)−1‖ over both contours. Proof. The smallest singular value is continuous and is bounded below by |ω|−‖ H‖ on the imaginary axis. Its infimum is attained and positive, and evaluation at zero gives ∆ax ≤ σmin (H) ≤ αH .Every eigenvalue λ has modulus at most αH and | Re λ| ≥ ∆ax , by testing a unit eigenvector at the shift i Im λ. Thus the rectangles enclose exactly their branches. On the inner edges, singular-value perturbation from the imaginary axis gives a gap of at least (1 − τ )∆ ax . On the remaining edges, |z| ≥ 2αH , so the gap is at least αH . These estimates give the inverse bound and, since 

|z| + αH ≤ (1 + 2 √2) αH , the upper bound in (B.10). An imaginary-axis minimizer ω∗ satisfies |ω∗| ≤ ‖ H‖ + ∆ ax ≤ 2αH . The points ±τ ∆ax + i ω∗

therefore lie on the inner edges, where singular-value perturbation bounds the gap above by (1 + 

τ )∆ ax . The shifted encoding scale is at least αH , proving the lower bound. A supplied positive lower bound on ∆ax may replace it in the contour locations and upper bounds. The matching lower bound uses the true gap. The rectangles provide a decay margin for time weights; their corners require a piecewise quadrature rule rather than the global analytic parametrization of Lemma A.4.

The proposition concerns the stated rectangles: an arbitrary contour may approach an eigenvalue and have a much larger factor. A conservative supplied gap likewise remains in the upper bound. For an ordinary discrete lift the separating set is the unit circle. The exterior branch requires a contour system that excludes the interior spectrum as well as the origin, where the inverse power weight is singular. 

Theorem B.6 (Unit-circle separation and annular contours) . Let S have no unit-circle spectrum and have nonempty interior and exterior branches. Let αS ≥ ‖ S‖, and set 

ηT = min  

> |z|=1

σmin (zI − S). (B.12) 

Then 0 < η T ≤ 1, αS > 1, and for 0 < τ < 1 the positively oriented contours 

|z| = 1 − τ η T, ∂{1 + τ η T < |z| < 3αS } (B.13) 

enclose exactly the interior and exterior branches, respectively. The annular inner boundary is oriented clockwise, so the exterior system has winding number zero about the closed unit disk. On the two circles adjacent to the unit circle, the smallest singular value is at least (1 − τ )ηT. The maximum generalized singularity factor over both contour systems is at most 

1 + αS + τ η T

(1 − τ )ηT

. (B.14) 

Proof. Compactness gives a positive gap. A unit eigenvector for an interior eigenvalue, tested at a nearest point on the unit circle, gives ηT ≤ 1; an exterior eigenvalue gives αS > 1. The same argument gives ∣∣|λ| − 1∣∣ ≥ ηT for every eigenvalue, so the proposed contours enclose exactly their branches. Singular-value perturbation from the unit circle proves the stated gap on its two adjacent circles, where the shifted scale is at most 1 + αS + τ η T. On |z| = 3 αS , the weighted inverse norm is at most 4αS /(3 αS − ‖ S‖) ≤ 2, which is bounded by (B.14). For S = SF , these contours give |zk| ≤ (1 − τ η T)k on the interior circle and |z−k| ≤ (1 + τ η T)−k

on the exterior system. Scaling the matrix moves the separating circle by the same scale. The 56 circle gap is separate from the singular value at zero, so the usual κ(S) alone does not supply it. For the DARE pencil, a fixed unit-circle contour gives directly 

RT,L = αM + αL

Hn =

−In An

is normal and acts on [v; v] and [v; −v] as An − In and An + In, respectively. Its spectrum {± 1 ± i}

Theorem B.4 bounds the rectangle factor above by a constant; every normalized inverse factor is at least one. For frequency T , the normal spectrum is {± 1 ± iT }, so the matrix norm and smallest singular value are both √1 + T 2, while the axis gap stays one. The rectangle upper bound is O(√1 + T 2).At its inner-edge point z = −1/2 + i T , the inverse norm is two and |z| + αH ≥ αH , giving the matching lower bound in (B.20). 57 Thus the construction factor and actual output normalization can stay bounded as the dimension grows. Normality and positive definite coefficients alone do not make the factor on the stated rectangles comparable to the usual condition number: varying the rotational frequency changes the relevant gap ratio. 

B.3 Coefficient bounds for CARE and DARE 

The preceding geometric identities identify the matrices that control spectral selection and recovery. We now bound them using supplied coefficient information. For the coefficient estimates below, assume 

‖A‖ ≤ a, g0I  G  ¯gI, q0I  Q  ¯qI, g0, q 0 > 0. (B.21) These are sufficient conditions for the bounds below, rather than additional assumptions on all problems in Section 2. In particular, singular G arising from a control input of deficient row rank is outside these coefficient estimates. The scalar bounds are supplied; the work needed to obtain them is separate from their use here. Positive stabilizing solutions and their complementary graphs are classical tools in algebraic Riccati theory [6, 18]. The following identity also applies to semidefinite solutions. It identifies the complete projector used in the spectral and initialization estimates below. 

Lemma B.8 (Complementary semidefinite graphs) . Let X, Y be Hermitian positive semidefinite matrices. The matrix I + Y X is invertible, the graphs ran[ I; X] and ran[ −Y ; I] are complementary, and the projector onto the first along the second is 

Π = [ I; X]( I + Y X )−1[I, Y ]. (B.22) 

Proof. Sylvester’s determinant identity gives 

det( I + Y X ) = det( I + X1/2Y X 1/2) > 0,

because X1/2Y X 1/2  0. Thus I + Y X is invertible. In the decomposition [f ; h] = [ I; X]s + [ −Y ; I]t,elimination of t gives (I + Y X )s = f + Y h . This proves complementarity and (B.22). For CARE, the same positivity bounds the solution norms and controls the generalized singu-larity factor through the imaginary-axis gap. 

Proposition B.9 (CARE coefficient bounds) . Under (B.21) , the equations 

A∗X + XA − XGX + Q = 0 , AY + Y A ∗ − Y QY + G = 0 (B.23) 

have positive definite stabilizing solutions. They satisfy 

‖X‖ ≤ uCARE := a + √a2 + g0 ¯qg0

,

‖Y ‖ ≤ vCARE := a + √a2 + q0 ¯gq0

For H = [ A, −G; −Q, −A∗], its stable projector is (B.22) , and 

∆ax ≥ min {g0, q 0}. (B.25) 58 Proof. Set Je = [0 , I; I, 0] . For every real Ω and every w = [ x; y],

Re (w∗Je(iΩ I − H)w) = x∗Qx + y∗Gy ≥ min {g0, q 0}‖ w‖2.

Since Je is unitary, Cauchy–Schwarz proves the gap bound and excludes imaginary-axis eigenvalues. With J = [0 , I; −I, 0] , the identity H∗J + JH = 0 pairs eigenvalues as λ, −λ, including algebraic multiplicities [40, Lemma 4]. The stable subspace therefore has dimension n.Along a stable trajectory ˙w = Hw , direct differentiation gives 

ddt (x∗y) = −x∗Qx − y∗Gy. 

Integration to infinity shows that x(0) ∗y(0) > 0 for every nonzero stable vector. In particular, no such vector is vertical. The stable subspace is consequently ran[ I; X]; the displayed positive quadratic forms imply X = X∗  0. Graph invariance gives the CARE and the Hurwitz matrix 

A − GX . Applying the same argument to (A∗, Q, G ) gives Y  0 and the Hurwitz matrix A∗ − QY .Evaluate the CARE at a unit eigenvector for the largest eigenvalue of X. This gives g0‖X‖2 ≤

2a‖X‖ + ¯ q, whose positive root is uCARE . The dual equation yields vCARE in the same way. Finally, 

H[I; X] = [ I; X]( A − GX ), H[−Y ; I] = [ −Y ; I]( QY − A∗).

The second reduced matrix has spectrum in the right half-plane. These are the two complete spectral subspaces, so Lemma B.8 gives the projector. If a bound (A+A∗)/2  hI is supplied, a in the two quadratic-root bounds can be replaced by h,with its sign unchanged even when h < 0. The gap in (B.25) supplies the finite separating contours of Theorem B.4. A further comparison with κ(H) still requires the gap ratio and normalization conditions of Proposition B.5; positive G, Q alone do not give that comparison. The supplied bound 

uCARE can be inserted directly in (F.3) and its precision budget; the actual projector normalization and any re-encoding cost remain. For DARE, a dual graph identifies the complete complementary branch of the pencil. B.4 Coefficient bounds for DRE and RR 

A dynamic construction additionally needs the coordinates of the initial graph in its selected branch. For DRE, Π+ annihilates the stable positive graph. For the forward recursion, Π> annihilates the complementary negative graph. We first bound the corresponding nondegeneracy factors, then connect them to the generalized singularity factors and the condition numbers used in the dynamic query bounds.

For RR, the branch assignment follows from the forward update itself. This distinction preserves the κ dependence in the dynamic complexity formulas while keeping the required coefficient, initialization, and time or step assumptions explicit. 64 C Weyl–LCHM construction of half-plane projectors 

Subsection 2.5 reduces the construction of ΠχR to approximating a scalar selector and implementing the resulting polynomial. We first prove the Weyl implementation in Proposition 2.11, then derive the polynomial degree required by Theorem 2.12. A Faber construction then combines scalar approximation with contour resolvent control, allowing matrices with arbitrary Jordan structure. The final subsection applies these exact-input results to approximate block-encodings by separating selector, matrix-input, and circuit errors. We use the normalized variables and selector notation of (2.26)–(2.29). 

C.1 Weyl realization and proof of the branch-block construction 

Proposition 2.11 turns a polynomial selector into an encoding of the Riesz block. We prove that im-plementation first; the degree estimates in the following subsections can then be converted directly into query bounds. We use the exact-input and coherent-access assumptions of Subsection 2.5. The angular identity itself holds for any contraction. Let ˜H denote the signal block of the supplied unit-normalized encoding, equal to ̂ H for exact inputs. Write the Weyl lift as

Proposition 2.11. Under the input and access assumptions above, let Sχ,r be the selector in (2.29) and satisfy ‖(Sχ,r (̂ H) − Πχ)R‖ ≤ ε/ 2, where ε > 0. Weyl–LCHM returns a block-encoding of ΠχR

with normalization αSχ,r αR, decoded error at most ε, using 

O(dS ) queries to the block-encodings of ̂ H and R. 

Proof. Choose N = 2 b, where b = dlog 2(dS +1) e. The construction above gives the exact signal block 

Sχ,r (̂ H)/α Sχ,r for ideal circuits. Composing with the encoding of R gives Sχ,r (̂ H)R at normalization 

αSχ,r αR. Implement the polynomial circuit to normalized block error at most ε/ (2 αSχ,r αR). Adding this implementation error to the selector error gives decoded error at most ε. The signal-processing sequence has O(dS ) signal calls, each using a constant number of controlled input or adjoint calls. Multiplication by R uses one further call. Coherent angular processing introduces no additional factor N in the query count. With b =

dlog 2(dS + 1) e, the angle register uses b qubits and the O(dS ) signal calls use O(dS log( dS + 1)) 

angle rotations in total. A direct composition uses at most aH + aR + b + 2 logical ancillas; the two extra qubits implement the Hermitian unitary and signal processing. Workspace internal to the oracles, reflections, and synthesis is additional. The cost now depends on which selector is chosen. We next determine its degree under the spectral assumptions of the main theorem. 

C.2 Proof of the sector query bound 

The implementation above applies to any selector with the required accuracy. Theorem 2.12 obtains an explicit cost by using the sector condition to choose that selector. C.3 Faber approximation with resolvent control 

Faber approximation removes the sector restriction. Combined with Lemma 2.10, it controls selector error for arbitrary Jordan structure through the squared-cap geometry and generalized singularity factor.

C.4 Input errors and precision 

The preceding constructions choose a selector for the exact lift and then implement it coherently. With approximate inputs, we keep that polynomial fixed and compare its values at the exact and decoded matrices. This avoids requiring the perturbed spectrum to satisfy the half-plane separation condition. Both matrices are contractions, so coefficient telescoping gives the sensitivity needed for this comparison.

Choose the degree to meet the selector budget, then evaluate the normalization and input sensitivity before fixing the remaining precisions. For the exact-input degree choice in (C.4), replace its tolerance ε by ε/ 2 to obtain selector error at most ε/ 4. signal calls and nrot = O(dS log( dS + 1)) angle rotations. Classical coefficient and phase computation, oracle and reflection costs, and subsequent graph recovery remain separate from these resource counts. 71 D Differential Riccati constructions and direct recovery 

We prove the graph identities used in Section 3, construct the four weighted blocks by finite-contour quadrature, and propagate their errors through the recovery algorithm. The final resource analysis separates the general implementation scales from the normalization assumptions used in Theorem 3.1. 

D.1 Hamiltonian flow and the decaying graph projector 

The graph-flow identity [5, Chap. 4] below requires only existence of the solution; it does not require spectral separation. 

Lemma D.1 (Hamiltonian graph flow) . For the data in Definition 2.3, write W(t) = etHDRE R0 =[W1(t); W2(t)] . On the promised existence interval, 

W(t) = 

[

I

P (t)

]

Y (t), ˙Y (t) = ( A − GP (t)) Y (t), Y (0) = I, (D.1) 

and Y (t) is invertible. Consequently W1(t) is invertible and P (t) = W2(t)W1(t)−1. Conversely, on any interval containing zero where W1(t) is invertible, this formula solves the DRE with initial value P0.Proof. The coefficient A − GP (t) is continuous on the existence interval, so its fundamental matrix 

Y (t) is invertible. Using the DRE to differentiate the product gives 

ddt (P (t)Y (t)) = ( −Q − A∗P (t)) Y (t).

Thus [I; P (t)] Y (t) solves the same linear system and has the same initial value as etHDRE R0. Unique-ness proves (D.1). For the converse, the two blocks of the linear flow satisfy 

˙W1 = AW1 − GW2, ˙W2 = −QW1 − A∗W2.

Differentiating W2W−11 gives −Q − A∗P − P A + P GP , and its initial value is P0.Existence supplies this qualitative invertibility, but does not imply spectral separation or full rank of Π+R0. The next proof uses these two additional conditions separately.

Lemma D.1 identifies the range with the solution graph on the promised interval. The factorization proof is valid for arbitrary Jordan structure. Using |g(zℓj )| ≤ 1 in (D.5) proves the bound on αg,R . Bounding the ideal integral by the same length estimate proves the target-norm bound. Taking αR = 1 or αR0 gives (D.14). All estimates of the weight used only t ≥ 0, so the stated uniformity follows. With exact inputs, allocate the remaining εT /2 to the finite-sum implementation in (D.6). If 

αg,R ≤ εT /2, the zero-block shortcut in Appendix A.4 already approximates the finite sum within that budget. For approximate inputs, Proposition A.5 supplies an additional error on the fixed ideal contours; choose quadrature, input, and implementation budgets whose sum is at most εT .The half-budget choice above then changes according to (D.10). The uniform absolute error does not remove the cost of preparing time-dependent coefficients or performing arithmetic with αH t. Node and weight preparation and the coherent reflection circuits have separate gate costs. The node count in (D.12) does not multiply the Hamiltonian query bound under the stated coherent access model. The supplied imaginary-axis lower bound also need not provide the required constant-factor inverse-norm estimate at every node. Finally, (D.14) controls these four input blocks for the specified rules. The initial-column pseudoinverse, products, sums, and recompression still determine the DGP normalization αE and the output normalization αP (t),as accounted for in Appendix D.4.

Theorem E.2 (Decaying graph projector) . For Problem 2, suppose that Π>R0 has full column rank. At the requested step k, set 

Ek = Π > + SkF Π<R0(Π >R0)+S−kF Π>.

Proof. Full column rank and Lemma E.1 give ran(Π >R0) = ran Π >. Thus (Π >R0)(Π >R0)+ acts as the identity on the exterior subspace, even though it need not equal the spectral projector Π>

on the whole space. Commutation of the spectral projectors with SF yields 

(S−kF Π>)( SkF R0) = Π >R0.

Splitting the propagated column into its two branches in (3.16) therefore gives 

Ek = SkF R0(Π >R0)+S−kF Π>, EkSkF R0 = SkF R0. (E.4) The first identity bounds the rank of Ek by n; the second proves that its range contains the rank-n

column SkF R0 and that it is the identity there. It is therefore idempotent, with the range stated in the theorem. It annihilates ran Π <, which has dimension n, so this subspace is exactly its kernel. The rank condition is equivalent to ran R0 ∩ ran Π < = {0}. It need not follow from positive semidefiniteness alone: for A = 2 and G = Q = P0 = 0 , the lift is diag(1 /2, 2) , the recursion is well defined, but Π>R0 = 0 . Under the stronger coefficient hypotheses of Proposition B.14, its lower bound does ensure full column rank for every P0  0. The branch correspondence in Lemma B.13 then identifies the exterior RR projector with the positive-graph DARE pencil projector; it does not identify the two interior projectors under the local conventions of the respective sections.

The choices in (E.12) give error at most εT /2 on the sole interior circle, or εT /4 on each of the two exterior circles. The first two strip widths are bounded below by constant multiples of ηT, whereas the third is constant, proving (E.13). This argument bounds absolute error and imposes no condition m > k ; it already includes all Fourier indices created by the power. At the real-parameter nodes, |g(zj )| ≤ 1 and βj ≤ cβ RRR /(|zj | + αSF ). The sum of the absolute geometric weights on a circle is ρ. Substitution in the LCU normalization proves (E.14) and (E.15). The same integral estimate gives ‖T ‖ ≤ 2αRRRR . These are upper bounds for the specified rule, not lower bounds on its raw normalization. Finally, apply Proposition 2.8 to the finite sum with implementation tolerance εT /2. Its inverse and LCU error allocation, proved in Appendix A.4, gives (E.16); the right-input encoding is used once. If the finite-sum normalization is below this tolerance, its zero approximation suffices instead. Adding the quadrature error proves the complete decoded-error claim. 86 The node count controls the node register, coefficient preparation, and arithmetic; it does not multiply the matrix-query count in (E.16). Computing the power weights still depends on the representation of k and the required working precision. Uniform absolute quadrature error therefore does not make all algorithmic resources independent of k. Likewise, access to the supplied lift encoding does not make the construction of A−1 from the original data free. Approximate input encodings are handled separately by Proposition A.5 on these fixed ideal contours, with an input-error budget added to quadrature and implementation errors. E.4 Stability and query complexity 

Use the coherent inverse and contour access of Section 2, and construct the four targets in (E.8) by Proposition E.4, or a supplied rule with the same error and query bounds. Write αquad

Its thresholds, implementation budget and query bound become (E.21) and (E.22). The approximate projector need not be idempotent. The remaining task is to obtain the required projector accuracy. The first pseudoinverse pre-serves the coordinates selected by the initial data; its singular value, rather than the solution bound, controls this step.

E.5 Approximate inputs and construction of the lift 

The matrix-query bound takes the lift and initial-column encodings as inputs. Their approximation errors must satisfy the same weighted target accuracy as the quadrature. If the lift is instead assembled from A, G, Q , its inverse of A also has a query cost. 

Proposition E.8 (RR construction from approximate inputs) . The same recovery result holds for approximate lift and initial-column encodings when the total error in each of the four weighted targets, including input, coefficient, quadrature, and implementation errors, meets (E.23) . Sufficient input conditions are those of Proposition A.5, applied to the fixed ideal contours in (E.7) . The query bounds use the actual perturbed normalizations and normalization-reduction overheads. For exact encodings of A, G, Q , an approximate inverse of A gives the lift accuracy εSF whenever

The factor RRR is evaluated using the resulting declared normalization of SF .91 F Algebraic Riccati constructions and proofs 

The algebraic algorithms encode the limiting Riesz projectors directly. We apply the graph recovery and stability results of Appendix D and specialize the quadrature arguments of Appendices D.3 and E.3. The Cayley alternative has additional inverse costs. Throughout, assume the exact input encodings, coherent node access, and uniform constant-factor inverse-norm estimates of Propositions 2.8 and 2.9. Contours are positively oriented and lie in the relevant resolvent set. Supply a bound on ‖X‖ before choosing the output scale and precision. Node preparation, arithmetic, and gate synthesis have separate costs. 

F.1 Limiting projectors and shared recovery 

Lemma F.1 (Limits of the decaying graph projectors) . For the CARE Hamiltonian, let Π−, Π+

be its two half-plane projectors. If Π−R0 has full column rank, the decaying graph projector for 

e−tHCARE R0 is 

Π− + e−tHCARE Π+R0(Π −R0)+etHCARE Π−. (F.1) 

It converges in norm to Π− as t → ∞ . For the unscaled RR lift and the full-rank initialization of Theorem E.2, Ek → Π> as k → ∞ . When A is invertible in the DARE pencil, SF = M −1L =(L−1M )−1, and this exterior projector equals the finite unit-disk pencil projector R<[1; M, L ].Proof. Apply the factorization in Theorem 3.2 to −H CARE , interchanging the two branches. Its correction term has norm at most 

‖e−tHCARE Π+‖ ‖ R0‖ ‖ (Π −R0)+‖ ‖ etHCARE Π−‖.

Both restricted exponentials tend to zero. This follows from their strict half-plane separation, including the polynomial factors of any Jordan blocks. In (3.16), the analogous factors SkF Π< and 

S−kF Π> both tend to zero, proving the discrete limit. If A is invertible, so are M, L ; inversion maps the inside spectrum of L−1M to the outside spectrum of SF , with the same spectral subspaces and projector. Equation (2.14) identifies it with the pencil Riesz operator. These are limits of projectors. Their graph property on any interval where the Riccati solution is finite follows from the existing graph-flow and recursion results. The algebraic algorithms encode the limit directly, so their query costs do not include an initialization pseudoinverse or a convergence time. For singular A, the DARE graph is identified directly from its regular pencil in Appendix F.3. For either limiting projector, Lemma D.3 and Proposition D.5 apply with P (t) = X and E(t) = Π. Under its nodewise smallness conditions, allocate quadrature, input, and implementa-tion errors a total budget δ, then use the same recovery proposition. No spectral assumption on the perturbed pencil over the entire contour is needed. 

F.4 Cayley construction under additional invertibility 

When L is invertible, the Cayley transformation converts the DARE disk selection to a half-plane selection [31]. The following implementation includes both the formation of L−1M and the shifted-inverse cost.

This appendix proves the application results of Section 4: classical feedback, the stable RPA compar-ison, and thermal-network input and parameter bounds. The proofs reuse the contour, inverse, and recovery estimates of Appendices F, D.2, and D.4, with the required instance-specific substitutions. Appendix G.5 gives the HBC matrix reconstruction and closed-loop validation.
